-correct family of thresholds for the GLR stopping rule. The stochastic initial times are

\forall a\in[K],\quad t_{a}^{\text{S}}(\delta)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\inf\left\{t\in\mathbb{N}\mid N_{t,a}\geq\max\left\{2,\left(\frac{\delta}{4(K-1)\zeta(s)}\right)^{1/s}\right\}\right\}\>.

(19)

Proof.

Let
s>1
and
\zeta
be the Riemann
\zeta
function. Let
\mathcal{T}_{n}
denotes the Student distribution with
n
degrees of freedom and
Q
its quantile function. We define a threshold
c_{a,b}(N_{t},\delta)
with value
+\infty
if
t<\max_{c\in\{a,b\}}t_{c}(\delta)
and otherwise
c_{a,b}(N_{t},\delta)=\max\left\{c(N_{t,a},\delta),c(N_{t,b},\delta)\right\}
.

Using Lemma
48
,
\delta
-correctness of the family of thresholds can be obtained directly by upper bounding (
6
). An initial time condition
t\geq\max_{c\in\{a,b\}}t_{c}(\delta)
is necessary for the threshold
(c_{a,b})_{a,b}
to be defined since they involve the quantiles of Student distribution. We obtain

t_{a}(\delta)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\inf\left\{t\in\mathbb{N}\mid N_{t,a}\geq\max\left\{2,\left(\frac{\delta}{4(K-1)\zeta(s)}\right)^{1/s}\right\}\right\}\>.

Let
a^{\star}=a^{\star}(\mu)
. A simple approach to control the sum of two terms is to control each term individually. Each individual term is a function of
\frac{\mu_{t,a}-\mu_{a}}{\sqrt{\tilde{\sigma}_{t,a}^{2}/N_{t,a}}}
which has a Student distribution, where
\tilde{\sigma}_{t,a}^{2}=\frac{N_{t,a}}{N_{t,a}-1}\sigma_{t,a}^{2}
is the unbiased variance. Let
\hat{\mu}_{t}
be the empirical mean of
t
standard Gaussian,
\hat{\sigma}_{t}^{2}=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\left(X_{s}-\hat{\mu}_{t}\right)^{2}
is the empirical variance and
\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}=\frac{t}{t-1}\hat{\sigma}_{t}^{2}
is its unbaised version. Using a union bound and the fact that
\tfrac{\hat{\mu}_{t}}{\sqrt{\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}/t}}\sim\mathcal{T}_{t-1}
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\exists t\geq\tilde{t}_{0}(\delta):\frac{\hat{\mu}_{t}}{\sqrt{\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}/t}}>\tilde{c}(t,\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t\geq t_{0}(\delta)}\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\hat{\mu}_{t}}{\sqrt{\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}/t}}>\tilde{c}(t,\delta)\right)\leq\frac{\delta}{4(K-1)}

where
\tilde{c}(t,\delta)=Q\left(1-\frac{\delta}{4(K-1)\zeta(s)t^{s}};\mathcal{T}_{t-1}\right)
. Using this result, direct computations yield that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\exists t\geq t_{0}(\delta):\>\exists a\neq a^{\star},\>\sum_{b\in\{a,a^{\star}\}}\frac{N_{t,b}}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{(\mu_{t,b}-\mu_{b})^{2}}{\sigma_{t,b}^{2}}\right)>\max_{b\in\{a,a^{\star}\}}c(N_{t,b},\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\exists t\geq t_{0}(\delta):\>\exists a\neq a^{\star},\>\exists b\in\{a,a^{\star}\},\>N_{t,b}\log\left(1+\frac{(\mu_{t,b}-\mu_{b})^{2}}{\sigma_{t,b}^{2}}\right)>c(N_{t,b},\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2(K-1)\mathbb{P}\left(\exists t\geq\tilde{t}_{0}(\delta):\>t\log\left(1+\frac{t}{t-1}\frac{\hat{\mu}_{t}^{2}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}}\right)>c(t,\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4(K-1)\mathbb{P}\left(\exists t\geq\tilde{t}_{0}(\delta):\frac{\hat{\mu}_{t}}{\sqrt{\tilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}/t}}>\sqrt{(t-1)\left(\exp\left(\frac{c(t,\delta)}{t}\right)-1\right)}\right)\leq\delta

where the last equation is obtained by choice of the stopping threshold

c(t,\delta)=t\log\left(1+\frac{1}{t-1}Q\left(1-\frac{\delta}{4(K-1)\zeta(s)t^{s}};\mathcal{T}_{t-1}\right)^{2}\right)\>.

(20)

Since it satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma
48
, this yields the desired result.
∎

EV-GLR stopping rule

Up to a log-transform the same arguments yield a family of thresholds for the EV-GLR stopping rule.
The proof of Lemma
50
is omitted since it is almost identical to the above.

Lemma 50
.

Let
s>1
and
\zeta
be the Riemann
\zeta
function. Let
(t_{a})_{a\in[K]}
as in (
19
). We define a threshold
c_{a,b}(N_{t},\delta)
with value
+\infty
if
t<\max_{c\in\{a,b\}}t_{c}(\delta)
and otherwise
c_{a,b}(N_{t},\delta)=\max\left\{c(N_{t,a},\delta),c(N_{t,b},\delta)\right\}
. Taking

c(t,\delta)=\frac{t}{t-1}Q\left(1-\frac{\delta}{4(K-1)\zeta(s)t^{s}};\mathcal{T}_{t-1}\right)^{2}

(21)

yields a
\delta
-correct family of thresholds for the EV-GLR stopping rule.

G.2
Box Thresholds

Before presenting the counterparts for the EV-GLR stopping rule, we first present the proof of Lemma
5
.

Proof.

Let
a\neq a^{\star}=a^{\star}(\mu)
.
Using Lemma
48
, we only need to exhibit threshold ensuring the required concentration behavior.
One way of obtaining such an upper bound is to maximize the above quantities under constraints obtained by our concentration results.
The form of the optimization is independent of the considered pair of arms
\{a,a^{\star}(\mu)\}
and of the time
t
(omitted in the following).
As we will see it only depends on
y_{b}=\frac{(\mu_{t,b}-\mu_{b})^{2}}{\sigma_{t,b}^{2}}
and
x_{b}=\frac{\sigma_{t,b}^{2}}{\sigma_{b}^{2}}
.

We can show that the family of thresholds in (
7
) is the solutions of an optimization problem.
Let
C,D\in(\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+})^{2}
and
N\in(\mathbb{N}^{\star})^{2}
,

maximize

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\{1,2\}}\frac{N_{b}}{2}\log\left(1+y_{b}\right)

such that

\displaystyle\forall b\in\{1,2\},\quad y_{b}\geq 0,\>x_{b}y_{b}\leq C_{b},\>x_{b}\geq D_{b}\>.

Since
y\mapsto\log\left(1+y\right)
is concave and increasing,
y\mapsto\sum_{b\in\{1,2\}}N_{b}\log\left(1+y_{b}\right)
is concave and increasing in each of its coordinates. Since the constraints and the objective are separate between each coordinate, the maximum is achieved at
\frac{C_{b}}{D_{b}}
and as value

\sum_{b\in\{1,2\}}\frac{N_{b}}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{C_{b}}{D_{b}}\right)\>.

To obtain the family of thresholds as in (
7
), we simply need to use concentration results to specify the constraints
C_{b}
and
D_{b}
, which differ depending on the considered pair of arms
\{a,a^{\star}(\mu)\}
and of the time
t
.
This can be done by combining the lower tail concentration on the empirical variance (Corollary
26
) and the upper and lower tail concentration of the empirical mean (Lemma
28
). By direct union bound, we have with probability greater than
1-\frac{\delta}{K-1}
, for all
b\in\{a,a^{\star}\}
and all
t\geq\max_{b\in\{a,a^{\star}\}}t_{b}(\delta)
,

\displaystyle(\mu_{t,b}-\mu_{b})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{b}^{2}\varepsilon_{\mu}(N_{t,b},\delta)\>,

\displaystyle\sigma_{t,b}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{b}^{2}(1-\varepsilon_{-,\sigma}(N_{t,b}-1,\delta))\>.

where
\varepsilon_{\mu}
,
\varepsilon_{-,\sigma}
and
t_{b}
are defined as in Lemma
5
.
Using Lemma
75
, this initial time condition ensures that
1-\varepsilon_{-,\sigma}(N_{t,b}-1,\delta)>0
. Since
\overline{W}_{0}
has values in
(0,1)
, we obtain that
\varepsilon_{-,\sigma}(t,\delta)\in(0,1)
.
Taking a union bound over
a\neq a^{\star}
concludes the proof.
∎

EV-GLR stopping rule

Up to a log-transform the same arguments yield a family of thresholds for the EV-GLR stopping rule (Lemma
50
).
The proof of Lemma
51
is omitted since it is almost identical to the above.

Lemma 51
.

Let
\varepsilon_{\mu}
,
\varepsilon_{-,\sigma}
and
(t_{a})_{a\in[K]}
as in Lemma
5
. We define a threshold
c_{a,b}(N_{t},\delta)
with value
+\infty
if
t<\max_{c\in\{a,b\}}t_{c}(\delta)
and otherwise

c_{a,b}(N_{t},\delta)=\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}\frac{N_{t,c}\varepsilon_{\mu}(N_{t,c},\delta)}{2(1-\varepsilon_{-,\sigma}(N_{t,c}-1,\delta))}\>.

(22)

This yields a
\delta
-correct family of thresholds for the EV-GLR stopping rule.

G.3
KL Thresholds

Thanks to Lemma
48
, it is sufficient to concentrate the summed KL divergence. Let
a^{\star}=a^{\star}(\mu)
. This can be done by Theorem
43
with
\mathcal{S}=\{a,a^{\star}\}
for all
a\neq a^{\star}
(and taking a bound over those
K-1
terms). For all
a\in[K]
, the additional initial time condition to obtain monotonicity rewrites

t_{a}(\delta)=\inf\left\{t\mid N_{t,a}>1+\max\left\{\frac{e^{s/\log\left(\frac{12(K-1)\zeta(s)}{\delta}\right)}}{1+\eta_{0}},\frac{e^{s/\left(\log\left(\frac{12(K-1)\zeta(s)}{\delta}\right)-\frac{1}{2(1+\eta_{1})}\right)}}{1+\eta_{1}}\right\}\right\}\>.

Numerically, we always observed that this was satisfied after initialization. Therefore, those terms have no impact.

G.4
BoB Thresholds

In Appendix
G.2
, we saw how to calibrate stopping threshold based on an optimization problem using concentration constraints.
Modifying the optimization problem therein, we can leverage Theorem
6
by adding a constraint.
While this argument requires doing the union bound over two concentration results (hence considering
\delta/2
), the added constraint can result in a smaller stopping threshold (hence faster stopping).

With our notations, for
b\in\{1,2\}
, the KL divergence rewrites as
\KL((\mu_{t,b},\sigma_{t,b}^{2}),(\mu_{b},\sigma^{2}_{b}))=\frac{1}{2}f(x_{b},y_{b})
with
f(x,y)=(1+y)x-1-\log(x)
. Since
\nabla^{2}f(x,y)=\begin{bmatrix}x^{-2}&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
is a positive semi-definite matrix on
(\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+})^{2}
,
f
is a convex function of
(\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+})^{2}
. Let
E>0
. The KL constraint from Theorem
6
is convex and can be expressed as

\sum_{b\in\{1,2\}}\frac{N_{b}}{2}f\left(x_{b},y_{b}\right)\leq E\>.

Since this constraint mixes the two coordinates, we can’t conclude by using the separation arguments.
To our knowledge, there is no closed form solution for the resulting optimization problem

maximize

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\{1,2\}}\frac{N_{b}}{2}\log\left(1+y_{b}\right)

such that

\displaystyle\forall b\in\{1,2\},\quad y_{b}\geq 0,\>x_{b}y_{b}\leq C_{b},\>x_{b}\geq D_{b}\>,

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\sum_{b\in\{1,2\}}\frac{N_{b}}{2}f\left(x_{b},y_{b}\right)\leq E\>.

However, as a maximization of a concave function under linear and convex inequalities, we can solve it numerically.
Corollary
7
is a direct consequence of the above manipulations and an union bound over concentration result.

EV-GLR stopping rule

The same ideas lead to Corollary
52
, which optimizes a different function under the same constraints. The resulting optimization problem is computationally faster since the objective is linear.
The proof of Corollary
52
is omitted since it is almost identical to the above.

Corollary 52
.

Let
f(x,y)=(1+y)x-1-\log(x)
for all
(x,y)\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})^{2}
. Let
(t_{a})_{a\in[K]}
in (
8
),
\varepsilon_{\mu},\varepsilon_{-,\sigma}
as in Lemma
5
and
(c_{b,a}^{\KL})_{b,a\in[K]}
in (
10
). The family of thresholds
c_{a,b}(N_{t},\delta)
with value
+\infty
if
t<\max_{c\in\{a,b\}}t_{c}(\delta/6)
and otherwise solution of the optimization problem

maximize

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}N_{t,c}y_{c}

such that

\displaystyle\forall c\in\{a,b\},\quad y_{c}\geq 0,\>x_{c}y_{c}\leq\varepsilon_{\mu}(N_{t,c},\delta/2),\>x_{c}\geq 1-\varepsilon_{-,\sigma}(N_{t,c}-1,\delta/2)\>,

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{2}\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}N_{t,c}f\left(x_{c},y_{c}\right)\leq c_{b,a}^{\KL}(N_{t},\delta/2)

yields a
\delta
-correct family of thresholds for the EV-GLR stopping rule.

G.5
Asymptotically Tight Thresholds

Among the class of
\delta
-correct family of thresholds for the GLR stopping rule, Theorem
9
suggests we should select the asymptotically tight ones since they yield an asymptotically optimal algorithm.

As observed empirically in Figure
1
(a), the Student and the box thresholds are not asymptotically tight since their slope in
\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
is higher than the ones of the KL and BoB thresholds. Theoretically, the arguments used in the proofs also justify why they couldn’t reach the
\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
. For the Student threshold, it comes from the fact that we consider the inequality
\{(I)+(II)>2\beta\}\subset\{(I)>\beta\}\cup\{(II)>\beta\}
, hence we could at most reach
2\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
. For the box threshold, it comes from the fact that we concentrate the term of each arm individually as well, hence we could also reach at most
2\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
(higher in practice).

EV-GLR stopping rule

Similar arguments could be used to study the
\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
-dependency of the families of thresholds derived for the EV-GLR stopping rule. However, since they are
\delta
-correct, they can’t be asymptotically tight. Otherwise, the Theorem
9
will contradict the asymptotic lower bound on the expected sample complexity as
T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)<T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
(Lemma
4
).

KL and BoB thresholds

In order to hope to achieve the
\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
dependency one should concentrate the whole sum, as done for the KL threshold. Since
\log\left(1+y\right)=\inf_{x>0}f\left(x,y\right)
, the KL constraint is also an upper bound on the solution of the optimization problem, i.e. the BoB thresholds are smaller than the KL thresholds
c_{b,a}^{\KL}(N_{t},\delta/2)
, hence they inherit their
\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
dependency. Therefore, to show the asymptotically tightness of both the KL and the BoB thresholds, we only need to study the KL family of thresholds. This result is established in Lemma
53
.

Lemma 53
.

The KL thresholds defined in (
10
) is an asymptotically tight family of thresholds.

Proof.

We analyze our threshold under a sampling rule that starts by pulling all arms
t(\delta)=\max\{t_{0}(\delta),t_{1}(\delta)\}
times, where

\displaystyle t_{1}(\delta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\min\left\{t\in\mathbb{N}\mid t>e^{1+W_{0}\left(\frac{2(1+\eta_{0})}{e}\left(\log\left(\frac{12(K-1)\zeta(s)}{\delta}\right)+s\log\left(1+\frac{\log(t)}{\log(1+\eta_{0})}\right)\right)-\frac{1}{e}\right)}\right\}\>,

\displaystyle t_{0}(\delta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\max\left\{\frac{e^{s/\log\left(\frac{12(K-1)\zeta(s)}{\delta}\right)}}{1+\eta_{0}},\frac{e^{s/\left(\log\left(\frac{12(K-1)\zeta(s)}{\delta}\right)-\frac{1}{2(1+\eta_{1})}\right)}}{1+\eta_{1}}\right\}\>.

After
Kt(\delta)
pulls, our threshold is finite for all arms.
We have
t_{0}(\delta)\to_{\delta\to 0}\frac{1}{1+\min\{\eta_{0},\eta_{1}\}}
.
Using that
W_{0}(x)\approx\log(x)-\log\log(x)
(Appendix
I
),
t_{1}(\delta)
is asymptotically equivalent to
\frac{2(1+\eta_{0})\log(1/\delta)}{\log\log(1/\delta)}
.
Therefore, this initial pulling count
t(\delta)
satisfies
\limsup_{\delta\to 0}t(\delta)/\log(1/\delta)=0
.

Our threshold is in fact a family of threshold functions
(c_{a,b}(N_{t},\delta))_{a,b\in[K]}
, which after that initialization, are defined by

\displaystyle c_{a,b}(N_{t},\delta)=4\overline{W}_{-1}\left(1+\frac{\log\frac{2\zeta(s)^{2}}{\delta}}{4}+\frac{s}{4}\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}\log(1+\log_{\gamma}N_{t,c})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}\log\left(\gamma R_{t,c}(\delta)\right)\right)\>.

Using concavity of
x\mapsto\log(1+\log_{\gamma}(x))
and
\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}N_{t,c}\leq t
, we have

\displaystyle c_{a,b}(N_{t},\delta)\leq 4\overline{W}_{-1}\left(1+\frac{\log\frac{2\zeta(s)^{2}}{\delta}}{4}+\frac{s}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{\log t/2}{\log\gamma}\right)+\log\left(\gamma R(t,\delta)\right)\right)\>,

where
R(t,\delta)=\max_{a\in[K]}R_{t,a}(\delta)
. Using the above, the concavity of
\overline{W}_{-1}
(Lemma
75
) yields for
t\geq Kt_{0}(\delta)

\displaystyle c_{a,b}(N_{t},\delta)

\displaystyle\leq 4\overline{W}_{-1}\left(1+\frac{1}{4}\log\left(\frac{2\zeta(s)^{2}}{\delta}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+4\left(\frac{s}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{\log t/2}{\log\gamma}\right)+\log\left(\gamma R(t,\delta)\right)\right)\overline{W}_{-1}^{\prime}\left(1+\frac{1}{4}\log\left(\frac{2\zeta(s)^{2}}{\delta}\right)\right)\>.

For all
x
for which it is defined,
\overline{W}_{-1}^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{1-1/\overline{W}_{-1}(x)}
. Let
c>1
, we have
\overline{W}_{-1}^{\prime}(x)\leq c\iff\overline{W}_{-1}(x)\geq\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{c}}
. Taking
x_{c}
such that
\overline{W}_{-1}(x_{c})=\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{c}}
, which is possible since it is a strictly increasing function with values in
[1,+\infty)
, Lemma
75
yields that
x_{c}\leq\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{c}}+\log\left(1-\frac{1}{c}\right)
. For
c=2
, we obtain
\overline{W}_{-1}^{\prime}(x)\leq 2
for all
x\geq 2-\log(2)
, hence
\overline{W}_{-1}^{\prime}\left(1+\frac{1}{4}\log\left(\frac{2\zeta(s)^{2}}{\delta}\right)\right)\leq 2
for all
\delta<\min\{1,\delta_{0}\}
where
\delta_{0}=2\zeta(s)^{2}e^{-4}
. Then, for all
\delta<\min\{1,\delta_{0}\}
,

\displaystyle c_{a,b}(N_{t},\delta)

\displaystyle\leq 4\overline{W}_{-1}\left(1+\frac{1}{4}\log\left(\frac{2\zeta(s)^{2}}{\delta}\right)\right)+8\log\left(\gamma R(t,\delta)\right)+4s\log\left(1+\frac{\log t/2}{\log\gamma}\right)\>.

We know that
4s\log\left(1+\frac{\log t}{\log\gamma}\right)=O(t^{\alpha})
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
and, using
\overline{W}_{-1}(x)=_{+\infty}x+\log(x)+o(1)
(Lemma
75
), that
4\overline{W}_{-1}\left(1+\frac{1}{4}\log\left(\frac{2\zeta(s)^{2}}{\delta}\right)\right)=\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)+o\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)
. To conclude the proof, we need to show that
\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\frac{8\log(R(t,\delta))}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq 1
.

The term
R(t,\delta)
is a data-dependent term quantifying the goodness of the local quadratic approximation for the KL (as a function of the natural parameters). Taking the notations from Theorem
6
and setting
\mu_{++,t,a}^{2}=\max\mu_{\pm,t,a}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle R(t,\delta)=\max_{a\in[K]}\frac{\sigma_{-,t,a}^{3}f_{-}\left(g(\sigma_{-,t,a}^{2},\mu_{++,t,a}^{2})\right)}{\sigma_{+,t,a}^{3}f_{+}\left(g(\sigma_{+,t,a}^{2},\mu_{++,t,a}^{2})\right)}

where
f_{\pm}(x)=\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1-x}}{\sqrt{x}}
and
g(x,y)=\frac{2x}{(x+2y+\frac{1}{2})^{2}}
. Using the definition of
\varepsilon_{\mu}
and
\varepsilon_{\pm,\sigma}
,we obtain directly that
\lim_{t\to+\infty}\varepsilon_{\mu}(t,\delta)=0
and
\lim_{t\to+\infty}\varepsilon_{\pm,\sigma}(t,\delta)=0
.

Let
a\in[K]
. For all
t
, define
i_{t,a}=\lfloor\log_{\gamma}N_{t,a}\rfloor
,
n_{t,a}=\gamma^{i_{t,a}}
,
\bar{t}_{a}=\inf\left\{t\mid N_{t,a}=n_{t,a}\right\}
. Using the law of large number, we also have that
\lim_{N_{t,a}\to+\infty}\mu_{\bar{t}_{a},a}=\mu_{a}
and
\lim_{N_{t,a}\to+\infty}\sigma^{2}_{\bar{t}_{a},a}=\sigma^{2}_{a}
. Chaining the limits, we obtain
\lim_{N_{t,a}\to+\infty}\mu_{++,t,a}^{2}=\mu_{a}^{2}
and
\lim_{N_{t,a}\to+\infty}\sigma_{\pm,t,a}=\sigma_{a}
. Since the functions
f_{\pm}
and
g
are continuous, we have shown for all
a\in[K]

\displaystyle\lim_{N_{t,a}\to+\infty}\frac{\sigma_{-,t,a}^{3}f_{-}\left(g(\sigma_{-,t,a}^{2},\mu_{++,t,a}^{2})\right)}{\sigma_{+,t,a}^{3}f_{+}\left(g(\sigma_{+,t,a}^{2},\mu_{++,t,a}^{2})\right)}=\frac{f_{-}\left(g(\sigma_{a}^{2},\mu_{a}^{2})\right)}{f_{+}\left(g(\sigma_{a}^{2},\mu_{a}^{2})\right)}

When
\delta\to 0
, the initialization yields
t_{0}(\delta)\to\infty
. Therefore, by using the above convergence and the fact that
N_{t,a}\geq t_{0}(\delta)
, there exists
\delta_{1}\in(0,\min\{1,\delta_{0}\})
such that for all
\delta\leq\delta_{1}
and all
t\geq Kt_{0}(\delta)
,

\displaystyle R(t,\delta)\leq 2\max_{a\in[K]}\frac{f_{-}\left(g(\sigma_{a}^{2},\mu_{a}^{2})\right)}{f_{+}\left(g(\sigma_{a}^{2},\mu_{a}^{2})\right)}\>,

which is a constant independent of
\delta
, hence
\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\frac{8\log(R(t,\delta))}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq 1
.
∎

Appendix H
Expected Sample Complexity

Theorem
9
gives asymptotic upper bound on the expected sample complexity of the algorithms using the GLR stopping rule.
Theorem
10
gives an impossibility results for the algorithms using the EV-GLR stopping rule, which is based on first deriving an upper bound on the expected sample complexity.
In Appendix
H.1
, we prove the upper bound for TaS with the GLR stopping rule and for EV-TaS with the EV-GLR stopping rule.
In Appendix
H.2
, we prove it for
\beta
-EB-TCI with the GLR stopping rule and for
\beta
-EB-EVTCI with the EV-GLR stopping rule.

H.1
Wrapped Track-and-Stop

Showing an asymptotic upper bound on the expected sample complexity of TaS and EV-TaS can be done with similar asymptotic arguments as when the variance is known
[
Garivier and Kaufmann 2016
]
.
The proof relies on two main ingredients.
First, a concentration result for
(\mu_{t},\sigma^{2}_{t})
is obtained thanks to the forced exploration (Lemma
54
proved in Appendix
H.1.1
).
Second, we use the Lemma 20 in
Garivier and Kaufmann 2016
, which ensures that the empirical allocation
\frac{N(t)}{t}
converges towards the optimal allocation being targeted.
While it corresponds to
w^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
for TaS, it is
w^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)
for EV-TaS (where the
\sigma^{2}
dependency is hidden by notation).

Without loss of generality and for the sake of simpler notations, we assume that the Gaussian bandit model with parameter
(\mu,\sigma^{2})\in(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+})^{K}
is such that
a^{\star}(\mu)=1
.

Track-and-Stop

Since the proofs share the same structure as in
Garivier and Kaufmann 2016
, we detail the one for TaS and highlight the differences for EV-TaS later.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
.
From the continuity of
(\mu,\sigma^{2})\mapsto w^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})

[
Degenne and Koolen 2019
]
, there exists
\xi_{1}=\xi_{1}(\varepsilon)\leq\frac{\min_{a\neq 1}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{a})}{4}
,
\xi_{2}=\xi_{2}(\varepsilon)>1
and
\xi_{3}=\xi_{3}(\varepsilon)\in(0,1)
such that

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\left(\bigtimes_{a\in[K]}\left[\mu_{a}-\xi_{1},\mu_{a}+\xi_{1}\right]\right)\times\left(\bigtimes_{a\in[K]}\left[\sigma^{2}_{a}\xi_{3},\sigma^{2}_{a}\xi_{2}\right]\right)

satisfies
\max_{a}\left|w^{\star}_{a}\left(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\sigma}^{2}\right)-w^{\star}_{a}(\mu,\sigma^{2})\right|\leq\varepsilon
for all
(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\sigma}^{2})\in\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}
.
Since
a^{\star}(\tilde{\mu})=1
for all
(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\sigma}^{2})\in\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}
, the empirical best arm is
\hat{a}_{t}=1
whenever
(\mu_{t},\sigma^{2}_{t})\in\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}
.
Let
T\in\mathbb{N}
,
h(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}T^{1/4}
and define the concentration event
\mathcal{E}_{T}=\bigcap_{t=h(T)}^{T}\left\{(\mu_{t},\sigma^{2}_{t})\in\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\right\}
.

The forced exploration ensures that each arm is drawn at least of order
\sqrt{t}
times at round
t
.
Thanks to concentration results on both
(\mu_{t},\sigma^{2}_{t})
, Lemma
54
upper bounds
\mathbb{P}_{\nu}\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\complement}\right)
.

Lemma 54
.

Let
T
such that
h(T)>\left(K+\frac{1}{1-\xi_{3}}\right)^{2}
. There exist two constants
B,C
(that depend on
(\mu,\sigma^{2})
and
\varepsilon
) such that

\mathbb{P}_{\nu}\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\complement}\right)\leq BT\exp\left(-CT^{1/8}\right)
.

Lemma
55
is exactly Lemma 20 in
Garivier and Kaufmann 2016
, hence the proof is omitted.

Lemma 55

(Lemma 20 in
Garivier and Kaufmann 2016
)
.

There exists a constant
T_{\varepsilon}
such that for
T\geq T_{\varepsilon}
, it holds that on
\mathcal{E}_{T}
, for either
C
Tracking or D-Tracking,

\forall t\geq\sqrt{T},\quad\max_{a\in[K]}\left|\frac{N_{t,a}}{t}-w^{\star}_{a}(\mu,\sigma^{2})\right|\leq 3(K-1)\varepsilon

On the event
\mathcal{E}_{T}
, it holds for
t\geq h(T)
that
\hat{a}_{t}=1
and the GLR rewrites

\displaystyle\min_{a\neq 1}Z_{a}(t)

\displaystyle=\min_{a\neq 1}\inf_{\lambda\in[\mu_{t,a},\mu_{t,1}]}\sum_{b\in\{1,a\}}\frac{N_{t,b}}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{\left(\mu_{t,b}-\lambda\right)^{2}}{\sigma_{t,b}^{2}}\right)=tg\left(\mu_{t},\sigma^{2}_{t},\frac{N_{t}}{t}\right)

where, for
(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\sigma}^{2})\in(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+})^{K}
such that
a^{\star}(\tilde{\mu})=1
and
\tilde{w}\in\triangle_{K}
, we introduced the function

\displaystyle g(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\sigma}^{2},\tilde{w})=\min_{a\neq 1}\inf_{\lambda\in[\tilde{\mu}^{a},\tilde{\mu}^{1}]}\sum_{b\in\{1,a\}}\frac{\tilde{w}_{b}}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{\left(\tilde{\mu}_{b}-\lambda\right)^{2}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}\right)

Recall that
a^{\star}(\tilde{\mu})=1
on
\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}
. We introduce
C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})=\underset{(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\sigma}^{2},\tilde{w})\in\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{\inf}g\left(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\sigma}^{2},\tilde{w}\right)
, where

\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(\mu,\sigma^{2})=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\times\left\{\tilde{w}\in\triangle_{K}\mid\left\|\tilde{w}-w^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})\right\|_{\infty}\leq 2(K-1)\varepsilon\right\}\>.

Using Lemma
55
, for
T\geq T_{\varepsilon}
,
on the event
\mathcal{E}_{T}
it holds that for every
t\geq\sqrt{T}
,
\min_{a\neq 1}Z_{a}(t)\geq tC_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
.

Let
\alpha\in[0,1)
,
\delta_{0}\in(0,1]
, functions
f,\bar{T}:(0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
C
as in the definition of an asymptotically tight family of thresholds.
In the following, we consider
\delta\leq\delta_{0}
and
T\geq\left\{\bar{T}(\delta),T_{\varepsilon}\right\}
. Using that for all
a\neq 1
,
Z_{a}(t)\geq\min_{a\neq 1}Z_{a}(t)\geq tC_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
and
c_{1,a}(N_{t},\delta)\leq f(\delta)+Ct^{\alpha}\leq f(\delta)+CT^{\alpha}
, we obtain on
\mathcal{E}_{T}
,

\displaystyle\min\left\{\tau_{\delta},T\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{T}+\sum_{t=\sqrt{T}}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\tau_{\delta}>t\}\leq\sqrt{T}+\sum_{t=\sqrt{T}}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\exists a\neq 1,\>Z_{a}(t)\leq c_{1,a}(N_{t},\delta)\}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{T}+\sum_{t=\sqrt{T}}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{tC_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})\leq f(\delta)+CT^{\alpha}\}\leq\sqrt{T}+\frac{f(\delta)+CT^{\alpha}}{C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}

Introducing

T_{0}(\delta)=\inf\left\{T\geq\bar{T}(\delta):\sqrt{T}+\frac{f(\delta)+CT^{\alpha}}{C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}\leq T\right\},

for every
T\geq\max\left\{T_{0}(\delta),\bar{T}(\delta),T_{\varepsilon}\right\}
, one has
\mathcal{E}_{T}\subseteq\left\{\tau_{\delta}\leq T\right\}
, therefore, by using Lemma
54
,

\mathbb{P}_{\nu}\left(\tau_{\delta}>T\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\nu}\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{c}\right)\leq BT\exp\left(-CT^{1/8}\right)

and

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\tau_{\delta}\right]\leq T_{0}(\delta)+\bar{T}(\delta)+T_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{T=1}^{\infty}BT\exp\left(-CT^{1/8}\right).

We now provide an upper bound on
T_{0}(\delta)
. Letting
\eta>0
and introducing the constant

\displaystyle D(\eta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\inf\{T\in\mathbb{N}:T-\sqrt{T}\geq T/(1+\eta)\}\leq 1+\left(1+\frac{1}{\eta}\right)^{2}

one has

\displaystyle T_{0}(\delta)

\displaystyle\leq D(\eta)+\inf\left\{T\geq\bar{T}(\delta)\mid f(\delta)+CT^{\alpha}\leq T\frac{C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{1+\eta}\right\}\>.

For all
\gamma>0
, there exists
T_{\alpha,\gamma}
(depending on
\mu,\sigma^{2}
) such that for all
T\geq T_{\alpha,\gamma}
,

T\frac{C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{1+\eta}-CT^{\alpha}\geq T\frac{C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{(1+\eta)(1+\gamma)}\>.

Then,

\displaystyle T_{0}(\delta)

\displaystyle\leq D(\eta)+\bar{T}(\delta)+T_{\alpha,\gamma}+\inf\left\{T\mid f(\delta)\leq T\frac{C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{(1+\eta)(1+\gamma)}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 1+D(\eta)+\bar{T}(\delta)+T_{\alpha,\gamma}+\frac{(1+\eta)(1+\gamma)}{C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}f(\delta)\>.

Dividing by
\log(1/\delta)
and taking limits, we get

\displaystyle\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\frac{T_{0}(\delta)}{\log(1/\delta)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1+\eta)(1+\gamma)}{C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}\>.

We obtain that for every
\eta,\varepsilon,\gamma>0
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\tau_{\delta}\right]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\frac{T_{0}(\delta)}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\frac{(1+\eta)(1+\gamma)}{C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}\>.

By continuity of
g
and by definition of
w^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
, we obtain
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})=T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})^{-1}
.
Letting
\eta
and
\varepsilon
go to zero yields

\displaystyle\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\tau_{\delta}\right]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})\>.

∎

Empirical Variance Track-and-Stop

We only highlight some differences since the proofs are similar.
We emphasize here that the notations
w^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)
,
T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)
and all the ones we will use below hide the dependency in
\sigma^{2}
to distinguish this from the quantity defined when the variance is assumed to be unknown.
While this might be unfortunate, we believe it eases greatly the notations and the highlight the difference between the two complexities.

Lemma 56
.

Using the EV-GLR stopping rule with an asymptotically tight family of thresholds, EV-TaS satisfies that, for all
\nu
with
|a^{\star}(\mu)|=1
,

\limsup_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\tau_{\delta}^{\text{EV}}\right]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)\>.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
.
Similarly, there exists
\xi_{1}\leq\frac{\min_{a\neq 1}(\mu_{1}-\mu^{a})}{4}
,
\xi_{2}>1
,
\xi_{3}\in(0,1)
such that
\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}
defined as above satisfies
\max_{a}\left|w^{\star}_{a}\left(\tilde{\mu}\right)-w^{\star}_{a}(\mu)\right|\leq\varepsilon
for all
(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\sigma}^{2})\in\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}
.
Let
T\in\mathbb{N}
,
h(T):=T^{1/4}
and define the concentration event
\mathcal{E}_{T}
as above.
Since Lemma
54
relies solely on forced exploration, its result still hold in that case.
A tracking result similar to Lemma
55
gives the existence of
T_{\varepsilon}
such that for
T\geq T_{\varepsilon}
, it holds that on
\mathcal{E}_{T}
,

\forall t\geq\sqrt{T},\quad\max_{a\in[K]}\left|\frac{N_{t,a}}{t}-w^{\star}_{a}(\mu)\right|\leq 3(K-1)\varepsilon

On the event
\mathcal{E}_{T}
, it holds for
t\geq h(T)
that
\hat{a}_{t}=1
and the EV-GLR rewrites

\displaystyle\min_{a\neq 1}Z^{\text{EV}}_{a}(t)

\displaystyle=\min_{a\neq 1}\inf_{\lambda\in[\mu_{t,a},\mu_{t,1}]}\sum_{b\in\{1,a\}}N_{t,b}\frac{\left(\mu_{t,b}-\lambda\right)^{2}}{2\sigma_{t,b}^{2}}=tg\left(\mu_{t},\sigma^{2}_{t},\frac{N_{t}}{t}\right)

where, for
(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\sigma}^{2})\in(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+})^{K}
such that
a^{\star}(\tilde{\mu})=1
and
\tilde{w}\in\triangle_{K}
, we introduced the function

\displaystyle g(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\sigma}^{2},\tilde{w})=\min_{a\neq 1}\inf_{\lambda\in[\tilde{\mu}^{a},\tilde{\mu}^{1}]}\sum_{b\in\{1,a\}}\tilde{w}_{b}\frac{\left(\tilde{\mu}_{b}-\lambda\right)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}

We introduce
C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})=\underset{(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\sigma}^{2},\tilde{w})\in\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{\inf}g\left(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\sigma}^{2},\tilde{w}\right)
, where

\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(\mu,\sigma^{2})=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\times\left\{\tilde{w}\in\triangle_{K}\mid\left\|\tilde{w}-w^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)\right\|_{\infty}\leq 2(K-1)\varepsilon\right\}\>.

By tracking, for
T\geq T_{\varepsilon}
, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{T}
it holds that for every
t\geq\sqrt{T}
,

\min_{a\neq 1}Z^{\text{EV}}_{a}(t)\geq tC_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
.
Let
T\geq\max\left\{\bar{T}(\delta),T_{\varepsilon}\right\}
.
As above, on
\mathcal{E}_{T}
, we have
\min\left\{\tau_{\delta}^{\text{EV}},T\right\}\leq\sqrt{T}+\frac{f(\delta)+CT^{\alpha}}{C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}
and we introduce
T_{0}(\delta)=\inf\left\{T\geq\bar{T}(\delta):\sqrt{T}+\frac{f(\delta)+CT^{\alpha}}{C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}\leq T\right\}
.
For every
T\geq\left\{T_{0}(\delta),\bar{T}(\delta),T_{\varepsilon}\right\}
, one has
\mathcal{E}_{T}\subseteq\left\{\tau_{\delta}^{\text{EV}}\leq T\right\}
, therefore, by using Lemma
54
,

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\tau_{\delta}^{\text{EV}}\right]\leq T_{0}(\delta)+\bar{T}(\delta)+T_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{T=1}^{\infty}BT\exp\left(-CT^{1/8}\right)\>.

Manipulations similar as above yield an upper bound on
T_{0}(\delta)
.
By continuity of
g
and by definition of
w^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)
, we obtain
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}C_{\varepsilon}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})=T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)^{-1}
.
Letting
\eta
and
\varepsilon
go to zero yields

\displaystyle\limsup_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\tau_{\delta}^{\text{EV}}\right]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)\>.

∎

We are now ready to prove the impossibility result for EV-TaS (Theorem
10
).

Proof.

Let
(c_{a,b})_{(a,b)\in[K]^{2}}
be an asymptotically tight family of thresholds and a problem independent constant
c_{0}>0
.
Combining EV-TaS with the EV-GLR stopping rule using
(c_{0}c_{a,b})_{(a,b)\in[K]^{2}}
yields an algorithm such that, for all
\nu
with
|a^{\star}(\mu)|=1
,

\limsup_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\tau_{\delta}^{\text{EV}}\right]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq c_{0}T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)\>.

Lemma
56
shows the above result for
c_{0}=1
, and generalizing to
c_{0}>0
is direct.
Suppose towards contradiction that the obtained algorithm is
\delta
-correct.
Therefore, using Lemmas
2
and
4
, we have shown that

T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})\leq\liminf_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\tau_{\delta}^{\text{EV}}\right]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\limsup_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\tau_{\delta}^{\text{EV}}\right]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq c_{0}T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)<c_{0}T^{\star}_{\beta}(\mu,\sigma^{2})\>.

For
c_{0}\in(0,1)
, the contradiction is direct.
For
c_{0}\geq 1
, we have shown that there is a problem independent constant
c_{0}>0
such that
T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})/T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)\leq c_{0}
.
This is a direct contradiction with Lemma
12
showing that there exists a sequence of instances
(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
|a^{\star}(\nu_{n})|=1
such that
\lim_{n\to+\infty}T^{\star}(\mu_{n},\sigma_{n}^{2})/T^{\star}_{\sigma_{n}^{2}}(\mu_{n})=+\infty
.
Therefore, the obtained algorithm is not
\delta
-correct.
∎

H.1.1
Proof of Lemma
54

Since
T
is such that
h(T)>\left(K+\frac{1}{1-\xi_{3}}\right)^{2}
, we have
h(T)\geq K^{2}
and
\xi_{3}<1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{h(T)}-K}
. In particular, for all
t\in\{h(T),\cdots,T\}
and all
s\in\{\sqrt{t}-K,\cdots,t\}
,
\xi_{3}+\frac{1}{s}<1
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{c}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=h(T)}^{T}\sum_{a=1}^{K}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(|\mu_{t,a}-\mu_{a}|\geq\xi_{1}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\sigma^{2}_{a,t}\geq\sigma^{2}_{a}\xi_{2}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\sigma^{2}_{a,t}\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}_{a}}{\xi_{2}}\right)\right]

By forced exploration, for
t\geq h(T)
one has
N_{t,a}\geq(\sqrt{t}-K/2)_{+}-1\geq\sqrt{t}-K
for every arm
a
. In the proof of Lemma 19 in
Garivier and Kaufmann 2016
it was shown using a union bound over time and Lemma
29
that, for all
t\in[h(T),T]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\mu_{t,a}-\mu_{a}|\geq\xi_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2e^{-(\sqrt{t}-K)\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}}{2\sigma_{a}^{2}}}}{1-e^{-\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}}{2\sigma_{a}^{2}}}}\>.

Since we use the same method to show our result on the variance, the proof for the mean is omitted.

Let
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{a,s}
be the empirical variance of the first
s
reward from arm
a
(such that
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{a,N_{t,a}}=\sigma^{2}_{a,t}
). We adopt their proof strategy to derive the equivalent upper bound on the concentration of the variance.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sigma^{2}_{a,t}\geq\sigma^{2}_{a}\xi_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\sigma^{2}_{a,t}\geq\sigma^{2}_{a}\xi_{2},N_{t,a}\geq\sqrt{t}-K\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=\sqrt{t}-K-1}^{t-1}\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{a,s+1}\geq\sigma^{2}_{a}\xi_{2}\right)\leq\sum_{s=\sqrt{t}-K-1}^{t-1}\exp\left(-\frac{s}{2}\left(h\left(\xi_{2}+\frac{1}{s}\right)-1\right)\right)

The first inequality is obtained by taking a union bound over the values of
N_{t,a}\in[\sqrt{t}-K,t]
. The second inequality is obtained by Corollary
27
with
x=\xi_{2}+\frac{1}{s}>1
. Using that
h(x)=x-\log(x)
and
\log(1+x)\leq x
, direct computations yield that

\displaystyle sh\left(\xi_{2}+\frac{1}{s}\right)=s\xi_{2}+1-s\log(\xi_{2}+\frac{1}{s})=sh(\xi_{2})+1-s\log(1+\frac{1}{s\xi_{2}})\geq sh(\xi_{2})+1-\frac{1}{\xi_{2}}\>,

\displaystyle\sum_{s=\sqrt{t}-K-1}^{t-1}\left(e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(h(\xi_{2})-1\right)}\right)^{s}\leq\frac{1}{1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(h(\xi_{2})-1\right)}}e^{-\frac{(\sqrt{t}-K-1)}{2}\left(h(\xi_{2})-1\right)}\>.

Putting those together, we obtain, for all
t\in[h(T),T]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sigma^{2}_{a,t}\geq\sigma^{2}_{a}\xi_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi_{2}}\right)}}{1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(h(\xi_{2})-1\right)}}e^{-\frac{(\sqrt{t}-K-1)}{2}\left(h(\xi_{2})-1\right)}\>.

The same manipulations using Corollary
27
with
x=\xi_{3}+\frac{1}{s}\in(0,1)
for all
s\in[\sqrt{t}-K,t]
and all
t\in[h(T),T]
(see above by choice of
T
), yield that, for all
t\in[h(T),T]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sigma^{2}_{a,t}\leq\sigma^{2}_{a}\xi_{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi_{3}}\right)}}{1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(h(\xi_{3})-1\right)}}e^{-\frac{(\sqrt{t}-K-1)}{2}\left(h(\xi_{3})-1\right)}\>.

Finally, letting

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}}{\max_{a\in[K]}\sigma_{a}^{2}},h\left(\xi_{2}\right)-1,h\left(\xi_{3}\right)-1\right\}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\sum_{a=1}^{K}\left(\frac{2e^{K\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}}{2\sigma_{a}^{2}}}}{1-e^{-\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}}{2\sigma_{a}^{2}}}}+\frac{e^{\frac{K-1}{2}\left(h(\xi_{2})-1\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi_{2}}\right)}}{1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(h(\xi_{2})-1\right)}}+\frac{e^{\frac{K-1}{2}\left(h(\xi_{3})-1\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi_{3}}\right)}}{1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(h(\xi_{3})-1\right)}}\right)\>,

one obtains

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{c}\right)\leq\sum_{t=h(T)}^{T}B\exp(-\sqrt{t}C)\leq BT\exp(-\sqrt{h(T)}C)=BT\exp\left(-CT^{1/8}\right)\>.

H.2
Wrapped
\beta
-EB-TCI

Showing an asymptotic upper bound on the expected sample complexity of
\beta
-EB-TCI and
\beta
-EB-EVTCI can be done with similar asymptotic arguments as when the variance is known
[
Shang et al. 2020
]
.
We will use the unified analysis of Top Two algorithms introduced in
Jourdan et al. 2022
, which highlights simple properties that the leader and the challenger should satisfy to obtain the desired upper bound.
While they introduced it for single-parameter exponential families and bounded distributions, it also allows to cope for our two-parameters setting.
The proof is composed of three steps: showing sufficient exploration of all arms, proving that the expectation of the convergence time towards the
\beta
-optimal allocation is finite and then concluding on the asymptotic upper bound.

To ensure sufficient exploration, we assume that
\min_{a\neq b}|\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}|>0
, i.e. all the arms have distinct means.
To our knowledge, this assumption is shared by all Top Two algorithms analysis.

H.2.1
\beta
-EB-TCI

Let
a^{\star}=a^{\star}(\mu)
. Since the proofs share the same structure as in
Jourdan et al. 2022
, we detail the one for
\beta
-EB-TCI and highlight the differences for
\beta
-EB-EVTCI.
The
\beta
-optimal allocation for Gaussian with unknown variance is defined as

\displaystyle w_{\beta}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})=\argmax_{w\in\triangle_{K},w_{a^{\star}}=\beta}\min_{a\neq a^{\star}}\inf_{u\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{b\in\{a^{\star},a\}}w_{b}\log\left(1+\frac{(\mu_{b}-u)^{2}}{\sigma_{b}^{2}}\right)\>.

In
Russo 2016
and
Jourdan et al. 2022
, they show the
\beta
-optimal allocation was unique for any single-parameter exponential families and for bounded distributions.
Since the proof only relies on strict convexity already shown in Appendix
C.2
, it is straightforward to see that
w_{\beta}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
is a singleton (Property 1 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
), denoted by
\{w^{\beta}\}
, such that
\min_{a\in[K]}w^{\beta}_{a}>0
.

Before delving into the specifics of the proof, Lemma
57
gathers concentration results on which the subsequent analysis heavily relies on.

Lemma 57
.

There exists a sub-Gaussian random variable
W_{\mu}
and a sub-exponential random variable
W_{\sigma}
, which are independent, such that almost surely for all
a\in[K]
and all
t
such that
N_{t,a}\geq 2
,

N_{t,a}|\mu_{t,a}-\mu_{a}|\leq W_{\mu}\log(e+N_{t,a})\quad\text{and}\quad\left|N_{t,a}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t,a}}{\sigma^{2}_{a}}-1\right)+1\right|\leq W_{\sigma}\log(e+N_{t,a})\>.

There exists a random variable
W_{0}=\max_{a\in[K]}W_{a}^{-1}
, independent of
W_{\mu}
, where
W_{a}/\sigma_{a}^{2}\sim\chi^{2}(n_{0}-1)
such that almost surely, for all
a\in[K]
and all
t
such that
N_{t,a}\geq n_{0}
,
N_{t,a}\sigma^{2}_{t,a}\geq W_{0}^{-1}
.
In particular,
W_{0}
admits a finite mean for
n_{0}\geq 4
and a finite variance for
n_{0}\geq 6
, and any random variable which is polynomial in both
W_{\sigma}
and
W_{\mu}
has a finite expectation.

Proof.

The existence of
W_{\mu}
is given by Lemma 73 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
.

Using Lemma
25
, we have for all
a\in[K]
and
t
such that
N_{t,a}\geq 2
,
N_{t,a}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t,a}}{\sigma_{a}^{2}}-1\right)+1=S_{N_{t,a}-1,a}
, where
S_{n,a}
is sub-exponential with
c=2
and
-S_{n,a}
is sub-exponential with
c=-2
and
S_{n-1,a}+n-1=\sum_{s\in[n-1]}Y_{s,a}^{2}
is such that
Y_{s,a}
are i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
Using Lemma 72 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
,
\sup_{N_{t,a}\geq 2}\frac{|S_{N_{t,a}-1,a}|}{\log(e+N_{t,a})}
is also sub-exponential.
By defining
W_{\sigma}
as the maximum over the finitely many arms of the above quantity, we obtain that
W_{\sigma}
is sub-exponential.

Since the increments
Y_{s,a}^{2}
are positive, we have for all
a\in[K]
and
t
such that
N_{t,a}\geq n_{0}

\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t,a}}{\sigma^{2}_{a}}=\frac{S_{N_{t,a}-1,a}+N_{t,a}-1}{N_{t,a}}=\frac{\sum_{s\in[N_{t,a}-1]}Y_{s,a}^{2}}{N_{t,a}}\geq\frac{Z_{a}}{N_{t,a}}\>,

where
Z_{a}=\sum_{s\in[n_{0}-1]}Y_{s,a}^{2}\sim\chi^{2}(n_{0}-1)
. Taking
W_{a}=\sigma^{2}_{a}Z_{a}
and the maximum over
[K]
yields the result.
As maximum of finitely many inverse-chi-squared distributions,
W_{0}
admits a finite mean for
n_{0}\geq 4
and a finite variance for
n_{0}\geq 6
.

For the independence property between
W_{\sigma}
and
W_{\mu}
and between
W_{0}
and
W_{\mu}
, this is a direct consequence of the fact that the empirical mean and empirical variance are independent.
Therefore, any random variable which is polynomial in both
W_{\sigma}
and
W_{\mu}
has a finite expectation.
∎

Using Lemma
57
, it is direct to obtain that, for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
N_{\varepsilon}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[N_{\varepsilon}]<+\infty
such that for all
a\in[K]
and
t
such that
N_{t,a}\geq N_{\varepsilon}
,
|\mu_{t,a}-\mu_{a}|\leq\varepsilon
and
\left|\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t,a}}{\sigma^{2}_{a}}-1\right|\leq\varepsilon
.

As in
Qin et al. 2017
,
Shang et al. 2020
,
Jourdan et al. 2022
, our goal is to upper bound the expectation of the
convergence time
.
For
\varepsilon>0
, the random variable
T^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}
quantifies the number of samples required for the empirical allocations
\frac{N_{t}}{t}
to be
\varepsilon
-close to
w^{\beta}
:

T^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\inf\left\{T\geq 1\mid\forall t\geq T,\>\left\|\frac{N_{t}}{t}-w^{\beta}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon\right\}\>.

(23)

Lemma
58
shows that a sufficient condition for asymptotic
\beta
-optimality is to show
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[T^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}]<+\infty
for all
\varepsilon
small enough.

Lemma 58
.

Let
(\delta,\beta)\in(0,1)^{2}
.
Assume that there exists
\varepsilon_{1}(\mu,\sigma^{2})>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1}(\mu,\sigma^{2})]
,
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[T^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}]<+\infty
.
Combining the GLR stopping rule (
4
) with an asymptotically tight family of threshold
yields an algorithm such that, for all
(\mu,\sigma^{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times(\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+})^{K}
with
|a^{\star}(\mu)|=1
,

\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\tau_{\delta}]}{\log\left(1/\delta\right)}\leq T_{\beta}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})\>.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
.
Let
c_{\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\min_{a\in[K]}w^{\beta}_{a}>0
and
\Delta=\min_{a\neq a^{\star}}|\mu_{a^{\star}}-\mu_{a}|>0
.
Let
\zeta>0
. By continuity of

(\mu,\sigma^{2},w)\mapsto\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}w_{c}\log\left(1+\frac{(\mu_{c}-x)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{c}}\right)\mathbf{1}\{\mu_{a}>\mu_{b}\}

on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times(\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+})^{K}\times\triangle_{K}
, there exists
\varepsilon_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle\max_{a\in[K]}\left|\frac{N_{t,a}}{t}-w^{\beta}_{a}\right|\leq\varepsilon_{2}\quad\text{,}\quad\max_{a\in[K]}\left|\mu_{t,a}-\mu_{a}\right|\leq\varepsilon_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\max_{a\in[K]}\left|\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t,a}}{\sigma^{2}_{a}}-1\right|\leq\varepsilon_{2}

\displaystyle\implies\quad

\displaystyle\max_{a\in[K]}|\mu_{t,a}-\mu_{a}|\leq\frac{\Delta}{4}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{t}\min_{a\neq a^{\star}}C_{t}(a^{\star},a)\geq\frac{1-\zeta}{T_{\beta}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}\>.

Choosing such a
\varepsilon_{2}
, we take
\varepsilon\in(0,\min\{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},c_{\beta}\})
.
By assumption, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[T^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}]<+\infty
, hence
\frac{N_{t,a}}{t}\geq w^{\beta}_{a}-\varepsilon\geq c_{\beta}
for all
a\in[K]
.

Let
N_{\varepsilon}
as described above (obtained with Lemma
57
). For all
t\geq c_{\beta}^{-1}N_{\varepsilon}
, we have
N_{t,a}\geq N_{\varepsilon}
for all
a\in[K]
, hence
\max_{a\in[K]}|\mu_{t,a}-\mu_{a}|\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{2}
and
\max_{a\in[K]}\left|\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t,a}}{\sigma^{2}_{a}}-1\right|\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{2}
.
Therefore, we have
\hat{a}_{t}\in\argmax_{a\in[K]}\mu_{t,a}=\argmax_{a\in[K]}\mu_{a}=a^{\star}
as
\max_{a\in[K]}|\mu_{t,a}-\mu_{a}|\leq\frac{\Delta}{4}
.

Let
\alpha\in[0,1)
,
\delta_{0}\in(0,1]
, functions
f,\bar{T}:(0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
C
as in the definition of an asymptotically tight family of thresholds.
In the following, we consider
\delta\leq\delta_{0}
.
Let
\kappa>0
. Let
T\geq\frac{1}{\kappa}\max\{T^{\varepsilon}_{\beta},c_{\beta}^{-1}N_{\varepsilon},\bar{T}(\delta)\}
.
Using the definition of the GLR stopping rule (
4
) with a family of asymptotically tight threshold, we have

\displaystyle\min\left\{\tau_{\delta},T\right\}\leq\kappa T+\sum_{t=\kappa T}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\tau_{\delta}>t\}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa T+\sum_{n=\kappa T}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{\exists a\neq a^{\star},\>C_{t}(a^{\star},a)\leq c_{a^{\star},a}(N_{t},\delta)\}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa T+\sum_{n=\kappa T}^{T}\mathbf{1}\{t\frac{1-\zeta}{T_{\beta}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}\leq f(\delta)+CT^{\alpha}\}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa T+\frac{T_{\beta}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{1-\zeta}\left(f(\delta)+CT^{\alpha}\right)\>.

Let
T_{\zeta}(\delta)
as

T_{\zeta}(\delta)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\inf\left\{T\geq 1\mid\frac{T_{\beta}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{(1-\zeta)(1-\kappa)}\left(f(\delta)+CT^{\alpha}\right)\leq T\right\}\>.

For every
T\geq\max\{T_{\zeta}(\delta),\frac{1}{\kappa}\max\{T^{\varepsilon}_{\beta},c_{\beta}^{-1}N_{\varepsilon},\bar{T}(\delta)\}
, we have
\tau_{\delta}\leq T
, hence

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\tau_{\delta}]\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[T^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}]+\frac{1}{\kappa c_{\beta}}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[N_{\varepsilon}]+\frac{1}{\kappa}\bar{T}(\delta)+T_{\zeta}(\delta)\>.

As
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[T^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}]+c_{\beta}^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[N_{\varepsilon}]<+\infty
and
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{\bar{T}(\delta)}{\log(1/\delta)}=0
, we obtain for all
\zeta,\kappa>0

\displaystyle\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\tau_{\delta}\right]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\frac{T_{\zeta}(\delta)}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\frac{T_{\beta}^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{(1-\zeta)(1-\kappa)}\>,

where the last inequality uses an inversion result.
Letting
\zeta
and
\kappa
go to zero yields the result.
∎

To upper bound the expected convergence time, as prior work we first establish sufficient exploration.
Given an arbitrary threshold
L\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
, we define the sampled enough set and its arms with highest mean (when not empty) as

S_{t}^{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\{a\in[K]\mid N_{t,a}\geq L\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{I}_{t}^{\star}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\argmax_{a\in S_{t}^{L}}\mu_{a}\>.

(24)

We define the highly and the mildly under-sampled sets

U_{t}^{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\{a\in[K]\mid N_{t,a}<L^{1/6}\}\quad\text{and}\quad V_{t}^{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\{a\in[K]\mid N_{t,a}<L^{5/6}\}\>,

(25)

where we used
L^{1/6}
and
L^{5/6}
instead of
\sqrt{L}
and
L^{3/4}
as done in
Shang et al. 2020
.
However,
Jourdan et al. 2022
noted the results would hold with
L^{\alpha_{1}}
and
L^{\alpha_{2}}
with
0<\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<1
.

Lemma
59
shows that the transportation cost is strictly positive and increases linearly.

Lemma 59
.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
with
\mu_{b}<\mu_{a}
.
There exists
L
with
\mathbb{E}[|L|^{\alpha}]<+\infty
for all
\alpha>0
and
D_{\nu}>0
such that for
N_{t,a}\geq L
and
N_{t,b}\geq L
,
C_{t}(a,b)>LD_{\nu}
, where
D_{\nu}>0
is a problem dependent constant.

Proof.

Suppose that
N_{t,a}\geq L
and
N_{t,b}\geq L
, for some
L
to be determined. First we get

\displaystyle C_{t}(a,b)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{L}{2}\mathbf{1}\{\mu_{t,a}>\mu_{t,b}\}\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}\log\left(1+\frac{(\mu_{t,c}-x)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{t,c}}\right)\>.

For any compact interval
\mathcal{I}_{C}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, the function defined by

(\mu,\sigma^{2})\mapsto\mathbf{1}\{\mu_{a}>\mu_{b}\}\inf_{x\in\mathcal{I}_{C}}\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}\log\left(1+\frac{(\mu_{c}-x)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{c}}\right)

is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times(\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+})^{K}
.
For
L
greater than some
L_{1}
with finite moments, we have
\mu_{t,a}>\mu_{t,b}
,
[\mu_{b}-\varepsilon,\mu_{a}+\varepsilon]\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{C}
and
(\mu_{t,a},\sigma^{2}_{t,a})
is
\varepsilon
-close to
(\mu_{a},\sigma^{2}_{a})
(and same thing for
(\mu_{b},\sigma^{2}_{b})
).
The continuity then gives that there exists
L
with finite moments such that

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}\log\left(1+\frac{(\mu_{t,c}-x)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{t,c}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\inf_{x\in\mathcal{I}_{C}}\sum_{c\in\{a,b\}}\log\left(1+\frac{(\mu_{c}-x)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{c}}\right)\>.

This is strictly positive since
\mu_{b}<\mu_{a}
due to the continuity and strict convexity properties holding for Gaussian with unknown variance.
∎

Lemma 60
.

Let
D_{\nu}>0
as in Lemma
59
.
Let
S_{t}^{L}
and
\mathcal{I}_{t}^{\star}
as in (
24
).
There exists
L_{4}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[(L_{4})^{\alpha}]<+\infty
for all
\alpha>0
such that if
L\geq L_{4}
, for all
t
such that
S_{t}^{L}\neq\emptyset
, for all
(a,b)\in\mathcal{I}_{t}^{\star}\times\left(S_{t}^{L}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{t}^{\star}\right)
, we have
C_{t}(a,b)\geq LD_{\nu}
.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
59
yields the result directly.
∎

Lemma
61
gives an upper bound on the transportation costs between a sampled enough arm and an under-sampled one.

Lemma 61
.

Let
S_{t}^{L}
as in (
24
). There exists
L_{5}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[(L_{5})^{\alpha}]<+\infty
for all
\alpha>0
such that for all
L\geq L_{5}
and all
t\in\mathbb{N}
,

\forall(a,b)\in S_{t}^{L}\times\overline{S_{t}^{L}},\quad C_{t}(a,b)\leq\frac{L}{2}\log\left(1+LW_{0}\left(D_{1}+4W_{\mu}\right)^{2}\right)\>,

where
D_{1}>0
is a problem dependent constant and
W_{0},W_{\mu}
are the random variables defined in Lemma
57
.

Proof.

Let
(a,b)\in S_{t}^{L}\times\overline{S_{t}^{L}}
(
a
is sampled more than
L
times,
b
is not). Taking
x=\mu_{t,a}
yields

\displaystyle C_{t}(a,b)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}N_{t,b}\log\left(1+\frac{(\mu_{t,b}-\mu_{t,a})^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{t,b}}\right)\leq\frac{L}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{(\mu_{t,b}-\mu_{t,a})^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{t,b}}\right)\>,

where we used that
b\in\overline{S_{t}^{L}}
and that
C_{t}(a,b)=0
when
\mu_{t,b}\geq\mu_{t,a}
.

By definition of
W_{0}
and
W_{\mu}
, we have for all
k\in[K]

\displaystyle\mu_{t,k}\leq\mu_{k}+W_{\mu}\log(e+N_{t,k})/N_{t,k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma^{2}_{t,k}

\displaystyle\geq W_{k}/N_{t,k}\>.

Then, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{(\mu_{t,b}-\mu_{t,a})^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{t,b}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{N_{t,b}}{W_{b}}\left(|\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}|+W_{\mu}\left(\frac{\log(e+N_{t,a})}{N_{t,a}}+\frac{\log(e+N_{t,b})}{N_{t,b}}\right)\right)^{2}\>.

Since
x\mapsto\frac{\log(e+x)}{x}
is decreasing on
\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+}
, we have
\frac{\log(e+N_{t,k})}{N_{t,k}}\leq 2
for
N_{t,k}\geq 1
. Then, since
W_{0}=\max_{a\in[K]}W_{a}^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{(\mu_{t,b}-\mu_{t,a})^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{t,b}}

\displaystyle\leq LW_{0}\left(|\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}|+4W_{\mu}\right)^{2}\>,

which yields the result by taking
D_{1}=\max_{a\neq b}|\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}|
.
∎

Lemma
62
shows that the EB leader satisfies the required condition on a leader to obtain sufficient exploration, i.e. Property 2 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
.

Lemma 62

(Lemma 17 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
)
.

Let
S_{t}^{L}
and
\mathcal{I}_{t}^{\star}
as in (
24
).
Let
L_{4}
in Lemma
60
.
Then, for all
L\geq L_{4}
, for all
n
such that
S_{t}^{L}\neq\emptyset
,
B_{t+1}^{\text{EB}}\in S_{t}^{L}
implies
B_{t+1}^{\text{EB}}\in\mathcal{I}_{t}^{\star}
.

Lemma
63
shows that the TCI challenger satisfies the required condition on a challenger to obtain sufficient exploration, i.e. Property 3 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
.
The proof of Lemma
63
resembles the proof of Lemma 21 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
.
The sole technical difference lies in the necessities of having an finite mean for
W_{0}
, which explains why we consider
n_{0}=4
for
\beta
-EB-TCI.

Lemma 63
.

Let
U_{t}^{L}
and
V_{t}^{L}
as in (
25
) and
\mathcal{J}_{t}^{\star}=\argmax_{a\in\overline{V_{t}^{L}}}\mu_{a}
.
There exists
L_{6}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[L_{6}]<+\infty
such that if
L\geq L_{6}
, for all
n
such that
U_{t}^{L}\neq\emptyset
,
B_{t+1}^{\text{EB}}\notin V_{t}^{L}
implies
A_{t+1}^{\text{TCI}}\in V_{t}^{L}\cup\left(\mathcal{J}_{t}^{\star}\setminus\left\{B_{t+1}^{\text{EB}}\right\}\right)
.

Proof.

Using Lemmas
60
,
61
and
62
, for all
L
larger than a random variable
L_{7}
with finite expectation,
B^{\text{EB}}_{t+1}\in\mathcal{J}_{t}^{\star}
and

\displaystyle\forall(a,b)\in\mathcal{J}_{t}^{\star}\times\left(\overline{V_{t}^{L}}\setminus\mathcal{J}_{t}^{\star}\right),\quad C_{t}(a,b)+\log N_{t,b}\geq L^{5/6}D_{\nu}+\frac{5}{6}\log L\>,

\displaystyle\forall(a,b)\in\overline{U_{t}^{L}}\times U_{t}^{L},\quad C_{t}(a,b)+\log N_{t,b}\leq\frac{L^{1/6}}{2}\log\left(1+L^{1/6}W_{0}\left(D_{1}+4W_{\mu}\right)^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{6}\log L\>.

Using that
\log(1+x)\leq\sqrt{x}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{L^{1/6}}{2}\log\left(1+L^{1/6}W_{0}\left(D_{1}+4W_{\mu}\right)^{2}\right)\leq\frac{L^{1/4}}{2}\sqrt{W_{0}}\left(D_{1}+4W_{\mu}\right)

Therefore, for
L\geq L_{8}:=W_{0}^{6/7}\left(\frac{D_{1}+4W_{\mu}}{2D_{\nu}}\right)^{12/7}+1
, we have that

L^{5/6}D_{\nu}+\frac{5}{6}\log L>\frac{L^{1/6}}{2}\log\left(1+L^{1/6}W_{0}\left(D_{1}+4W_{\mu}\right)^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{6}\log L\>.

Let
L_{6}=\max\{L_{7},L_{8}\}

Therefore, at least one under-sampled arm has transportation cost lower than all the ones that are much sampled.
This implies that
A_{t+1}^{\text{TCI}}\in V_{t}^{L}\cup\left(\mathcal{J}_{t}^{\star}\setminus\left\{B_{t+1}^{\text{EB}}\right\}\right)
.
Then, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[L_{6}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[L_{7}]+1+\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[W_{0}^{6/7}\right]\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\left(\frac{D_{1}+4W_{\mu}}{2D_{\nu}}\right)^{12/7}\right]<+\infty\>,

The first inequality and the last strict inequality are obtained by Lemma
57
, thanks to the independence of
W_{0}
and
W_{\mu}
, the fact that
W_{0}
has finite mean since
n_{0}\geq 4
and that polynomial of
W_{\mu}
have finite expectation.
∎

Since Properties 2 and 3 of
Jourdan et al. 2022
are satisfied (Lemmas
62
and
63
), Lemma
64
holds for
\beta
-EB-TCI on instances such that
\min_{a\neq b}|\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}|>0
.

Lemma 64

(Lemma 7 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
)
.

There exist
N_{0}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[N_{0}]<+\infty
such that for all
t\geq N_{0}
and all
a\in[K]
,
N_{t,a}\geq\left(t/K\right)^{1/6}
.

Now that we have proved sufficient exploration (Lemma
64
), we will show convergence towards the
\beta
-optimal allocation.
Lemma
65
shows that the EB leader satisfies the required condition on a leader to obtain convergence, i.e. Property 5 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
.

Lemma 65

(Lemma 18 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
)
.

There exists
N_{6}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[N_{6}]<+\infty
such that for all
t\geq N_{6}
,
B_{t+1}^{\text{EB}}=a^{\star}
.

For all
a\in[K]
and all
t>t_{0}
, let
\psi_{t,a}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\mathbb{P}_{\mid(t-1)}[a_{t}=a]
be the probability of sampling arm
a
at time
t
and
\Psi_{t,a}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\sum_{s\in[t]}\psi_{s,a}
its cumulative sum.

Using the EB leader, Lemma
66
shows that the TCI challenger satisfies the required condition on a challenger to obtain convergence towards the
\beta
-optimal allocation, i.e. Property 6 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
.
The proof of Lemma
66
is very similar to the proof of Lemma 22 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
, hence we omit certain details.

Lemma 66
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. There exists
N_{7}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[N_{7}]<+\infty
such that for all
n\geq N_{7}
and all
a\neq a^{\star}(\mu)
,
\Psi_{t,a}/t\geq w_{a}^{\beta}+\varepsilon
implies that
A_{t+1}^{\text{TCI}}\neq a
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
65
, we know that
B_{t+1}^{\text{EB}}=a^{\star}
for all
t\geq N_{6}
.
By definition of
A_{t+1}^{\text{TCI}}
and algebraic manipulation,
A_{t+1}^{\text{TCI}}\neq a
is implied by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\left(C_{t}(a^{\star},a)-\min_{b\neq a^{\star}}C_{t}(a^{\star},b)\right)>\frac{\log(tK)}{2t}\>.

In Appendix
C.2
, we obtain the desired regularity properties: joint continuity, strict convexity, equality at the equilibrium.
Therefore, by using the same proof as the one of Lemma 20 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
, there exists
C_{\nu}>0
and
N_{7}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[N_{7}]<+\infty
such that for all
t\geq N_{7}
and all
a\neq a^{\star}
,

\frac{\Psi_{t,a}}{t}\geq w_{a}^{\beta}+\varepsilon\quad\implies\quad\frac{1}{t}\left(C_{t}(a^{\star},a)-\min_{b\neq a^{\star}}C_{t}(a^{\star},b)\right)\geq C_{\nu}\>.

Since
\frac{\log(tK)}{2t}\to_{\infty}0
, there exists a deterministic
N_{8}
such that for all
n\geq N_{8}
,
\frac{\log(tK)}{2t}<C_{\nu}
. Therefore, for all
n\geq\tilde{N}_{7}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\max\{N_{8},N_{7}\}
and all
a\neq a^{\star}
,
\Psi_{t,a}/t\geq w_{a}^{\beta}+\varepsilon
implies that
A_{t+1}^{\text{TCI}}\neq a
.
Since
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\tilde{N}_{7}]=N_{8}+\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[N_{7}]<+\infty
, this concludes the proof.
∎

Since there is sufficient exploration (Lemma
64
) and Properties 5 and 6 of
Jourdan et al. 2022
are satisfied (Lemmas
65
and
66
), Lemma
67
holds for
\beta
-EB-TCI on instances such that
\min_{a\neq b}|\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}|>0
.

Lemma 67

(Lemma 10 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
T_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}
as in (
23
). Then,
\beta
-EB-TCI satisfies
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[T_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}]<+\infty
.

Combining Lemmas
67
and
58
concludes the proof for
\beta
-EB-TCI.

H.2.2
\beta
-EB-EVTCI

Let
a^{\star}=a^{\star}(\mu)
. Similarly, the
\beta
-optimal allocation for Gaussian with known variance is defined as

\displaystyle w^{\star}_{\sigma^{2},\beta}(\mu)=\argmax_{w\in\triangle_{K},w_{a^{\star}}=\beta}\min_{a\neq a^{\star}}\inf_{u\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{b\in\{a^{\star},a\}}w_{b}\frac{(\mu_{b}-u)^{2}}{\sigma_{b}^{2}}\>,

is a singleton, denoted by
\{w^{\beta}\}
, such that
\min_{a\in[K]}w^{\beta}_{a}>0
.

Lemma 68
.

Using the EV-GLR stopping rule with an asymptotically tight family of thresholds,
\beta
-EB-EVTCI) satisfies that, for all
\nu
with
\min_{a\neq b}|\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}|>0
,

\limsup_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\tau_{\delta}^{\text{EV}}\right]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq T^{\star}_{\beta}(\mu,\sigma^{2})\>.

For
\varepsilon>0
, the random variable
\bar{T}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}
quantifies the number of samples required for the empirical allocations
\frac{N_{t}}{t}
to be
\varepsilon
-close to
w^{\beta}
:

\bar{T}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}\inf\left\{T\geq 1\mid\forall t\geq T,\>\left\|\frac{N_{t}}{t}-w^{\beta}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon\right\}\>.

(26)

Lemma
69
shows that a sufficient condition to obtain an upper bound on the asymptotic expected sample complexity is to show
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\bar{T}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}]<+\infty
for
\varepsilon
small enough.
The proof of Lemma
69
is omitted since it is almost identical to the one of Lemma
58
.

Lemma 69
.

Let
\delta,\beta\in(0,1)
.
Assume that there exists
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1}]
,
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\bar{T}^{\varepsilon}_{\beta}]<+\infty
.
Combining the EV-GLR stopping rule (
5
) with an asymptotically tight family of threshold
yields an algorithm such that, for all
(\mu,\sigma^{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times(\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+})^{K}
with
|a^{\star}(\mu)|=1
,

\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\tau^{\text{EV}}_{\delta}]}{\log\left(1/\delta\right)}\leq T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2},\beta}(\mu)\>.

Lemma
70
is obtained similarly as Lemma
60
, hence we omit the proof.

Lemma 70
.

Let
S_{t}^{L}
and
\mathcal{I}_{t}^{\star}
as in (
24
).
There exists
L_{4}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[(L_{4})^{\alpha}]<+\infty
for all
\alpha>0
such that if
L\geq L_{4}
, for all
t
such that
S_{t}^{L}\neq\emptyset
, for all
(a,b)\in\mathcal{I}_{t}^{\star}\times\left(S_{t}^{L}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{t}^{\star}\right)
, we have
C^{\text{EV}}_{t}(a,b)\geq LD_{\mu}
, where
D_{\nu}>0
is a problem dependent constant.

Lemma
71
is obtained similarly as Lemma
61
, hence we omit the proof.

Lemma 71
.

Let
S_{t}^{L}
as in (
24
). There exists
L_{5}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[(L_{5})^{\alpha}]<+\infty
for all
\alpha>0
such that for all
L\geq L_{5}
and all
t\in\mathbb{N}
,

\forall(a,b)\in S_{t}^{L}\times\overline{S_{t}^{L}},\quad C^{\text{EV}}_{t}(a,b)\leq\frac{L^{2}}{2}W_{0}\left(D_{1}+4W_{\mu}\right)^{2}\>,

where
D_{1}>0
is a problem dependent constant and
W_{0},W_{\mu}
are the random variables defined in Lemma
57
.

Lemma
72
is obtained similarly as Lemma
63
.
The sole technical difference lies in the necessities of having an finite variance for
W_{0}
, which explains why we consider
n_{0}=6
for
\beta
-EB-EVTCI.

Lemma 72
.

Let
U_{t}^{L}
and
V_{t}^{L}
as in (
25
) and
\mathcal{J}_{t}^{\star}=\argmax_{a\in\overline{V_{t}^{L}}}\mu_{a}
.
There exists
L_{6}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[L_{6}]<+\infty
such that if
L\geq L_{6}
, for all
n
such that
U_{t}^{L}\neq\emptyset
,
B_{t+1}^{\text{EB}}\notin V_{t}^{L}
implies
A_{t+1}^{\text{EVTCI}}\in V_{t}^{L}\cup\left(\mathcal{J}_{t}^{\star}\setminus\left\{B_{t+1}^{\text{EB}}\right\}\right)
.

Proof.

Using Lemmas
70
,
71
and
62
, for all
L
larger than a random variable
L_{7}
with finite expectation,
B^{\text{EB}}_{t+1}\in\mathcal{J}_{t}^{\star}
and

\displaystyle\forall(a,b)\in\mathcal{J}_{t}^{\star}\times\left(\overline{V_{t}^{L}}\setminus\mathcal{J}_{t}^{\star}\right),\quad

\displaystyle C_{t}(a,b)+\log N_{t,b}\geq L^{5/6}D_{\mu}+\frac{5}{6}\log L\>,

\displaystyle\forall(a,b)\in\overline{U_{t}^{L}}\times U_{t}^{L},\quad

\displaystyle C_{t}(a,b)+\log N_{t,b}\leq\frac{L^{1/3}}{2}W_{0}\left(D_{1}+4W_{\mu}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{6}\log L\>.

Therefore, for
L\geq L_{8}:=W_{0}^{2}\left(\frac{D_{1}+4W_{\mu}}{2D_{\nu}}\right)^{4}+1
, we have that

L^{5/6}D_{\mu}+\frac{5}{6}\log L>\frac{L^{1/3}}{2}W_{0}\left(D_{1}+4W_{\mu}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{6}\log L\>.

Let
L_{6}=\max\{L_{7},L_{8}\}
.
Therefore, at least one under-sampled arm has transportation cost lower than all the ones that are much sampled.
This implies that
A_{t+1}^{\text{EVTCI}}\in V_{t}^{L}\cup\left(\mathcal{J}_{t}^{\star}\setminus\left\{B_{t+1}^{\text{EB}}\right\}\right)
.
Then, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[L_{6}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[L_{7}]+1+\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[W_{0}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\left(\frac{D_{1}+4W_{\mu}}{2D_{\nu}}\right)^{4}\right]<+\infty\>.

The first inequality and the last strict inequality are obtained by Lemma
57
, thanks to the independence of
W_{0}
and
W_{\mu}
, the fact that
W_{0}
has finite variance since
n_{0}\geq 6
and that polynomial of
W_{\mu}
have finite expectation.
∎

Since Properties 2 and 3 of
Jourdan et al. 2022
are satisfied (Lemmas
62
and
72
), Lemma
64
holds for
\beta
-EB-EVTCI on instances such that
\min_{a\neq b}|\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}|>0
.

Lemma
73
is obtained similarly as Lemma
66
, hence we omit the proof.

Lemma 73
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. There exists
N_{7}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[N_{7}]<+\infty
such that for all
n\geq N_{7}
and all
a\neq a^{\star}(\mu)
,
\Psi_{t,a}/t\geq w_{a}^{\beta}+\varepsilon
implies that
A_{t+1}^{\text{EVTCI}}\neq a
.

Since there is sufficient exploration (Lemma
64
) and Properties 5 and 6 of
Jourdan et al. 2022
are satisfied (Lemmas
65
and
73
), Lemma
74
holds for
\beta
-EB-EVTCI on instances such that
\min_{a\neq b}|\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}|>0
.

Lemma 74

(Lemma 10 in
Jourdan et al. 2022
)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\bar{T}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}
as in (
26
). Then,
\beta
-EB-EVTCI satisfies
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\bar{T}_{\beta}^{\varepsilon}]<+\infty
.

Combining Lemmas
74
and
69
concludes the proof of the asymptotic upper bound on the expected sample complexity of
\beta
-EB-EVTCI, i.e. Lemma
68
.

In Appendix
C.1
, we explained that
T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2},\beta}(\mu)
and
T^{\star}_{\beta}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
satisfied inequalities like the ones between
T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)
and
T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
in (
2
).
Similar to Lemma
12
, we can show that there exists a sequence of instances
(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\min_{b\neq a}|\mu_{n,a}-\mu_{n,b}|>0
such that
\lim_{n\to+\infty}T^{\star}_{\beta}(\mu_{n},\sigma_{n}^{2})/T^{\star}_{\sigma_{n}^{2},\beta}(\mu_{n})=+\infty
.
Therefore, we can conclude the impossibility result for
\beta
-EB-EVTCI (Theorem
10
) with the same arguments as for EV-TaS in Appendix
H.1
.

Appendix I
The Lambert
W
Function

The Lambert
W
function is implicitly defined by the equation
W(x)e^{W(x)}=x.
It defines two main branches
W_{-1}
(negative) and
W_{0}
(positive).

•

W_{-1}
, defined on
[-e^{-1},0)
, is decreasing and
W_{-1}(-e^{-1})=-1
.

•

W_{0}
, defined on
[-e^{-1},+\infty)
, is increasing and
W_{0}(-e^{-1})=-1
.

The function
W_{0}
satisfies for all
x\geq e
,
W_{0}\left(e^{x}\right)\leq x
and

\frac{\log\log(x)}{2\log(x)}\leq W_{0}(x)-(\log(x)-\log\log(x))\leq\frac{e}{e-1}\frac{\log\log(x)}{\log(x)}\>.

Lambert’s branches are involved in the inversion of
h(x)=x-\log(x)
. When
x\geq 1
, it involves the negative branch. When
x\leq 1
, it involves the negative part of the positive branch. To make the notations clearer, we define for all
x\geq 1

\overline{W}_{-1}(x)=-W_{-1}(-e^{-x})\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{W}_{0}(x)=-W_{0}(-e^{-x})\>.

(27)

Lemma
75
gather useful properties on
\overline{W}_{-1}
and
\overline{W}_{0}
that we will use.

Lemma 75
.

(1)
For
x\geq 1
, let
h(x)=x-\log(x)
. Then,

\displaystyle\forall y\geq 1,\qquad y\leq h(x)\>\iff\>\begin{cases}\overline{W}_{-1}\left(y\right)\leq x&\text{if }x\geq 1\\
\overline{W}_{0}\left(y\right)\geq x&\text{if }x\in(0,1]\end{cases}\>,

\displaystyle\forall\delta>0,\forall c>0,\qquad e^{-c\left(h(x)-1\right)}\leq\delta\>\iff\>\begin{cases}\overline{W}_{-1}\left(1+\frac{1}{c}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\leq x&\text{if }x>1\\
\overline{W}_{0}\left(1+\frac{1}{c}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\geq x&\text{if }x\in(0,1)\end{cases}\>,

\displaystyle\forall x>1,\qquad\exp\left(-x+e^{-x}\right)\leq\overline{W}_{0}(x)\leq\exp\left(-x+e^{1-x}\right)\>,

\displaystyle\forall x>1,\qquad x+\log(x)\leq\overline{W}_{-1}(x)\leq x+\log(x)+\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}\right\}\>,

\displaystyle\forall u>1,\forall t>1,\qquad\overline{W}_{0}\left(1+\frac{u}{t}\right)\geq\frac{1}{t}\>\iff\>t\geq\exp\left(1+W_{0}\left(\frac{u-1}{e}\right)\right)\>.

(2)
The function
\overline{W}_{-1}
is increasing and strictly concave on
(1,+\infty)
. The function
\overline{W}_{0}
is decreasing and strictly convex on
(1,+\infty)
. In particular,

\forall x>1,\qquad(\overline{W}_{0})^{\prime}(x)=\left(1-\frac{1}{\overline{W}_{0}(x)}\right)^{-1}\>\text{ and }\>(\overline{W}_{-1})^{\prime}(x)=\left(1-\frac{1}{\overline{W}_{-1}(x)}\right)^{-1}\>.

Proof.

(1)
Let
y\geq 1
and
x\in(0,1]
. We obtain

\displaystyle\overline{W}_{0}(y)\geq x\iff W_{0}(-e^{-y})\leq-x\iff-e^{-y}\leq-xe^{-x}\iff y\leq x-\log(x)

where the second equivalence uses that
-e^{-y}=W_{0}(-e^{-y})e^{W_{0}(-e^{-y})}
,
y\mapsto ye^{y}
is increasing on
[-1,+\infty)
and
W_{0}(x)
has values on
[-1,0)
for
x\in[-e^{-1},0)
. Let
x\in(0,1)
,
\delta,c>0
. Then,

\displaystyle\overline{W}_{0}\left(1+\frac{1}{c}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\geq x

\displaystyle\iff 1+\frac{1}{c}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\leq h(x)\iff\exp\left(-c\left(h(x)-1\right)\right)\leq\delta

Let
x>1
and
f(x)\in(0,1)
. Then, we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{W}_{0}\left(x\right)\geq f(x)

\displaystyle\iff x\leq f(x)-\log(f(x))

For
f(x)=e^{-x+e^{-x}}
, we have
x\leq f(x)-\log(f(x))\iff e^{-x}\geq 0
, hence this condition holds and
\overline{W}_{0}\left(x\right)\geq f(x)
.
For
f(x)=e^{-x+e^{1-x}}
, we have
x\leq f(x)-\log(f(x))\iff x\leq 1
, hence this condition doesn’t hold for
x>1
, hence
\overline{W}_{0}\left(x\right)\leq f(x)
.

For
\overline{W}_{-1}(y)
, the same arguments yield the three results, which were first proven in Lemma A.1 and A.2 of
Degenne 2019
).

We denote
v=\frac{u-1}{t}>0
. Since
t>1
, direct manipulations show that

\displaystyle\overline{W}_{0}\left(1+\frac{u}{t}\right)\geq\frac{1}{t}\iff 1+\frac{u}{t}\leq\frac{1}{t}-\log\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\iff v+\log(v)\leq\log\left(\frac{u-1}{e}\right)

\displaystyle\iff ve^{v}\leq\frac{u-1}{e}\iff v\leq W_{0}\left(\frac{u-1}{e}\right)\iff t\geq\frac{u-1}{W_{0}\left(\frac{u-1}{e}\right)}=e^{1+W_{0}\left(\frac{u-1}{e}\right)}\

The equivalence introducing
W_{0}
uses that for
\alpha=\frac{u-1}{e}>0
,
W_{0}(\alpha)e^{W_{0}(\alpha)}=\alpha
,
y\mapsto ye^{y}
is increasing on
[-1,+\infty)
and
v>0
. The last equality uses that
e^{W_{0}(x)}=\frac{x}{W_{0}(x)}
.

(2)
Let
W
denote
W_{0}
or
W_{-1}
and
\overline{W}(x)=-W(-e^{-x})
. It is known (by implicit derivation) that
W^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{z+e^{W(z)}}
for
z\neq-e^{-1}
. Using that
e^{W(z)}=\frac{z}{W(z)}
, this yields that
zW^{\prime}(z)=\left(1+\frac{e^{W(z)}}{z}\right)^{-1}=\left(1+\frac{1}{W(z)}\right)^{-1}
. For
x\neq 1
, using the above with
z=-e^{-x}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{W}^{\prime}(x)=-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left(W(-e^{-x})\right)=-e^{-x}W^{\prime}(-e^{-x})=\left(1+\frac{1}{W(-e^{-x})}\right)^{-1}=\left(1-\frac{1}{\overline{W}(x)}\right)^{-1}

Since
W_{0}(-e^{-x})\in(-1,0)
for all
x>1
(positive branch on
(-e^{-1},0)
), we have
\overline{W}_{0}(x)\in(0,1)
, hence
\overline{W}_{0}^{\prime}(x)<0
for
x>1
.
Therefore,
\overline{W}_{0}
is decreasing on
(1,+\infty]
.
Using that
\overline{W}^{\prime}(x)=\left(1-\frac{1}{\overline{W}(x)}\right)^{-1}
for
x\neq 1
, we obtain that
\overline{W}^{\prime}_{0}
is increasing on
(1,+\infty]
, hence strictly convex.
The same arguments yield that
\overline{W}_{-1}
is increasing and strictly concave on
(1,+\infty]
.
∎

Lemma
76
was proven in
Degenne 2019
. It is needed when using the peeling method.

Lemma 76

(Lemma A.3 in
Degenne 2019
)
.

For
a,b\geq 1
, the minimal value of
f(\eta)=(1+\eta)(a+\log(b+\frac{1}{\eta}))
is attained at
\eta^{\star}
such that
f(\eta^{\star})\leq 1-b+\overline{W}_{-1}(a+b)
. If
b=1
, then there is equality.

Appendix J
Implementation Details and Additional Experiments

Implementations details are given in Appendix
J.1
.
Supplementary experiments are provided in Appendix
J.2
.

J.1
Implementation Details

We detail below the most relevant implementation details, both regarding the sampling rules and the considered family of thresholds.

Stopping thresholds

The implementation of the different families of thresholds require to specify the hyper-parameters
(s,\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\gamma)
.
Based on the obtained formulas, we see that there is a trade-off in their choice.
Smaller values of
(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})
yield close to ideal dependency in
\delta
, at the cost of increasing the dependency in
t
.
Smaller values of
s
yield close to ideal dependency in
t
, at the cost of increasing the constant term.
Smaller values of
\gamma
yield more frequent update of the approximation term error (i.e. the ratio of eigenvalues), at the cost of increasing the dependency in
t
.
Those updates are responsible for stair-step shaped curves of the KL and (EV-)BoB thresholds.
For reasonable choices of the parameters, the relative performance of the thresholds are not changed.
We conduct our experiments with
s=2
,
\gamma=1.2
,
\eta_{0}=\eta_{1}=\log\left(1/\delta\right)^{-1}
(as
\delta<1
).

Optimal allocation oracles

To compute the optimal allocation in TaS, we perform nested binary searches to solve the optimization problem described in Theorem
13
.
The outer binary search is done on
y\in[0,\min_{a\neq a^{\star}}d_{a^{\star}}(\mu_{a}))
.
The inner binary searches are done to compute
x_{a}(y)
for all
a\in[K]
.
To obtain
\lambda(x)
, we compute the (at most three) real solutions of the third order polynomial equation, and then return the one minimizing the function of interest. For EV-TaS, the procedure is similar as the one implemented in
Garivier and Kaufmann 2016
, and it is significantly faster as
\lambda(x)
has a closed-form solution.

Sampling rules

As recommended in
Jourdan et al. 2022
, we implemented the
\beta
-EB-TCI algorithm with
\beta=0.5
.
This is a deterministic instance of Top Two algorithm with an efficient implementation since it can re-use computations done in the stopping and recommendation rules.

Based on
Degenne et al. 2019
, we consider DKM with one learner on
\triangle_{K}
instead of
K
learners.
For unknown variances, the wrapped DKM algorithm is obtained by considering adapted transportation costs (and the corresponding alternatives).
To cope for an additional uncertainty due to the unknown variance, the bonus
\frac{\log(t)}{N_{t,a}}
is multiplied by
1+2\frac{\log(t)}{N_{t,a}}
.
This heuristic is legitimated by our box concentration.
The implementation of EV-DKM is direct by plugging in the empirical variance.
Based on
Wang et al. 2021
, the wrapped algorithms FWS and EV-FWS are obtained similarly.

FHN2 refers to the procedure 2 in
Fan et al. 2016
, whose pseudo-code (see Algorithm
J.1
) was given in
Hong et al. 2021
.
FHN
2
is an elimination strategy which repeatedly samples all arms until only one arm is left.
For all
a\in[K]
, let
\bar{X}_{n,a}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t\in[n]}X_{t,a}
where
(X_{t,a})_{t\in[n]}
are i.i.d. observations from
\nu_{\mu_{a},\sigma^{2}_{a}}
.
Its elimination mechanism compares the pairwise statistics
t_{b,a}(n)(\bar{X}_{n,b}-\bar{X}_{n,a})
to an elimination threshold
g_{b,a}(t_{b,a}(n),\delta)
, where the effective time
t_{b,a}(n)=n/S_{b,a}(n)
is defined with the empirical variances of pairwise comparison between arms
b
and
a

S_{b,a}(n)=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{t\in[n]}\left(X_{t,b}-X_{t,a}-\bar{X}_{n,b}+\bar{X}_{n,a}\right)^{2}\>.

The calibration of the elimination threshold, which is done by simulations arguments and continuous-time approximations, yields

g_{b,a}(x,\delta)=\sqrt{(x+1)\left(2\log\left(\frac{K-1}{2\delta}\right)+\log(x+1)\right)}\>.

The choice of the initial time
n_{0}
impacts the empirical performance of the algorithm, yet few practical guidelines were given.
In our experiments we took
n_{0}=\max\{2,10\log(1/\delta)\}
.

{algorithm}

[ht]

FHN
2

Let
I=[K]
and
n=n_{0}
(user-specified)

Sample
n_{0}
observations from each arm

\While
|I|>1

Compute
S_{b,a}(n)
and set
t_{b,a}(n)=n/S_{b,a}(n)
for all
b\neq a

Update active arms,
I\leftarrow I\setminus\left\{b\mid\forall a\neq b,\>t_{b,a}(n)(\bar{X}_{n,b}-\bar{X}_{n,a})<-g_{b,a}(t_{b,a}(n),\delta)\right\}

Pull each arm once, and set
n\leftarrow n+1

Return the unique arm in
I

In
Fan et al. 2016
, they also propose the procedure
1
, which we refer to as FHN
1
.
The main difference is that FHN
1
uses the empirical variances obtained after initialization, meaning it uses
t_{b,a}(n)=n/S_{b,a}(n_{0})
instead of
t_{b,a}(n)=n/S_{b,a}(n)
.
While one can argue that this wastes precious information, FHN
1
enjoys better theoretical guaranty.
The impact of then choice of
n_{0}
is more important for FHN
1
since
S_{b,a}(n_{0})
is used for elimination.

Reproducibility

Our code is implemented in
Julia 1.7.2
, and the plots are generated with the
StatsPlots.jl
package.
Optimizations are performed based on the
JuMP.jl
optimization package.
In particular, we use the Ipopt non-linear solver
[
Wächter and Biegler 2006
]
, available in
Ipopt.jl
, and the
LambertW.jl
package to compute the Lambert
W
function.
Other dependencies are listed in the
Readme.md
.
The
Readme.md
file also provides detailed julia instructions to reproduce our experiments, as well as a
script.sh
to run them all at once.
The general structure of the code (and some functions) is taken from the
tidnabbil
library.
2
2

2

This library was created by
Degenne et al. 2019
, see https://bitbucket.org/wmkoolen/tidnabbil. No license were available on the repository, but we obtained the authorization from the authors.

J.2
Supplementary Experiments

In Appendix
J.2.1
, we perform simulations on the characteristic times
T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
and
T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)
.
In Appendix
J.2.2
, we compare family of stopping thresholds for the EV-GLR stopping rule.

J.2.1
Characteristic Times

We perform numerical simulations to compare
T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
and
T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)
.
Based on the inequalities (
2
), we will compare
\frac{T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)}
and its upper bound
\frac{d(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{\log(1+d(\mu,\sigma^{2}))}
on
\frac{T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)}
, where
d(\mu,\sigma^{2})=\max_{a\neq a^{\star}(\mu)}\frac{(\mu_{a^{\star}(\mu)}-\mu_{a})^{2}}{\min\{\sigma_{a}^{2},\sigma_{a^{\star}(\mu)}^{2}\}}
.
Taking
K=2
, we consider the canonical instance
\mu=(0,-\Delta)
and
\sigma^{2}=(1,r)
, hence
d(\mu,\sigma^{2})=\frac{\Delta^{2}}{\min\{1,r\}}
, and instantiate it by default with
\Delta=0.2
and
r=0.5
.
We perform
10000
simulations for varying
\Delta
and
r
.

Figure 3:
Evolution of
\frac{T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)}
as a function of (a)
\Delta
for
r=0.5
and (b)
r
for
\Delta=0.2
.

Figure
3
empirically confirms that
\frac{d(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{\log(1+d(\mu,\sigma^{2}))}
is an upper bound on
\frac{T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)}
, which is always above
1
.
It shows that
\frac{T^{\star}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{T^{\star}_{\sigma^{2}}(\mu)}
is close to one as long as the gap is not too large.
Moreover, the upper bound
\frac{d(\mu,\sigma^{2})}{\log\left(1+d(\mu,\sigma^{2})\right)}
has the same behavior, except when the variance of the optimal arm is significantly larger than the one of the sub-optimal arm.
In this particular regime, our upper bound appears to be loose.

J.2.2
Thresholds

Using the same experimental setup as in Section
3.3
, we perform numerical simulations to compare the family of thresholds derived for the EV-GLR stopping rule: EV-Student (Lemma
50
), EV-Box (Lemma
51
) and EV-BoB (Corollary
52
).

Figure 4:
Threshold for (
5
) as a function of (a)
\log\left(1/\delta\right)
for
t=5000
and (b)
t
for
\delta=0.01
.

Figure
4
is the equivalent of Figure
1
and similar messages can be extracted from it.
However, for the EV-GLR stopping rule, the
\delta
-correct family of thresholds can be asymptotically tight, otherwise Theorem
9
would contradict the asymptotic lower bound.
The Student threshold in (
21
) has poor performance in
t
.
While the box threshold in (
22
) yields better performance in
t
, it comes at a worse dependency in
\log\left(1/\delta\right)
(higher slope).
The BoB threshold in Corollary
52
has the good performance in
t
of the box threshold and the same dependency in
\log\left(1/\delta\right)
as the Student threshold.

Figure 5:
Thresholds as a function of (a)
\log\left(1/\delta\right)
for
t=5000
and (b)
t
for
\delta=0.01
.

Figure
5
groups Figures
1
and
4
together.
By comparing the thresholds obtained by using the same method, all the thresholds for the EV-GLR stopping rule are above the ones obtained for the GLR stopping rule.
Given the ordering of the statistic (Lemma
16
), this was expected.
